We will concentrate on the axion domain walls, since they are our main focus of the thesis and since we already introduced cosmic strings.

Kinks and axion domain walls

A
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
Goldstone model with
V=\frac{\lambda}{4}(\phi^{2}-\eta^{2})^{2}
admits kink stable solutions with

\phi_{\rm kink}(z)=\eta\,\tanh\!\Big(\frac{z}{\delta_{\rm kink}}\Big),

(5.4.6)

where

\delta_{\rm kink}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\lambda\eta^{2}}},

(5.4.7)

which is localized and centered at
x=0
, taking values
\phi=-\eta
for
x\rightarrow-\infty
and
\phi=+\eta
for
x\rightarrow+\infty
.

Similarly, a sine-Gordon soliton comes from an analogous theory with a periodic potential of the form

V(\phi)=2\lambda\eta^{4}\Big[1-\cos{\Big(\phi/\eta\Big)}\Big],

(5.4.8)

which is the same form of istanton potential.
The analytic soliton solution is

\phi(x)=4\eta\arctan{\exp{\Big( \sqrt{\lambda} \eta x \Big)}},

(5.4.9)

for which we display a plot in Fig.
5.5
.

Figure 5.5:
Plot of the configuration of equation (
5.4.9
) in normalized units, interpolating between the two domains with
\phi=0
and
\phi=2\pi
.

All these solutions are remarkably non-dissipative, time-independent, localized and have finite energy.
Their stability is a consequence of a topological conservation law, with each defect state having a conserved quantum number.
For the one-dimensional case, the topological current is

j^{\mu}=\epsilon^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\phi.

(5.4.10)

Indeed, it is trivially conserved, since it is the divergence of an antisymmetric tensor. However, it does not arise from a continuous symmetry, then it is not conserved by Noether’s theorem.
It has an associated conserved charge
N

N=\int dx\,j^{0}=\phi(x)|_{x\rightarrow+\infty}-\phi(x)|_{x\rightarrow-\infty}.

(5.4.11)

showing that it is the presence of a soliton with
\phi
in different vacua at
x\rightarrow\pm\infty
to give rise to a non-zero charge
N
which gives it the classical stability.

Furthermore, they can be boosted up to arbitrary velocities thanks to the Lorentz invariance of the theory.

For example, in the instanton case of our interest, we have a boosted profile along the wall normal

a(\vec{x},t)=f_{a}\Big[2\pi k+4\arctan e^{\gamma m_{a}\hat{n}\cdot(\vec{x}-\vec{v}t)}\Big],

(5.4.12)

with a thickness of the domain wall

\delta_{\rm DW}\sim(\gamma m_{a})^{-1}.

(5.4.13)

The rest wall tension is

\sigma_{\rm DW}=8\,m_{a}f_{a}^{2}.

(5.4.14)

We show more on this configuration in Ref.
[
122
]
.

The stress–energy for a static planar wall is similarly to the case treated by Ref.
[
172
]

T^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(z)=\rho(z)\,\mathrm{diag}(1,-1,-1,0),\qquad\rho_{\rm kink}(z)=4m_{a}^{2}f_{a}^{2}\,\sech^{2}\!\Bigg(\frac{z}{\delta_{\rm DW}}\Bigg),

(5.4.15)

The Newtonian limit of relativistic Poisson’s equation
[
172
,
197
]
, coming from a stress-energy which is of the form of a perfect fluid, apart from a factor
\sech^{2}\!\Bigg(\frac{z}{\delta_{\rm kink}}\Bigg)
which localizes the wall distribution, is of the form

\nabla^{2}\Phi_{\text{grav}}=4\pi G(\rho+p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3})=-4\pi G\rho.

(5.4.16)

This means that we have a counterintuitive result, where an infinite flat domain wall repels a gravitational test particle rather than attracting it, and two infinite flat domain walls repel each other gravitationally.
This strange gravitational behaviour is only related to assuming an infinite planar wall, and the spatial dimensions are limited by the Hubble radius in Cosmology.

If we work again in the wall rest frame,

T^{\mu}{}_{\nu}(z^{\prime})=\rho(z^{\prime})\,\mathrm{diag}(1,-1,-1,0),\qquad z^{\prime}=\gamma(z-vt),\ \gamma=(1-v^{2})^{-1/2}.

(5.4.17)

Boosting along
z
, the direction normal to the wall, gives

T^{\prime 00}=\gamma^{2}\rho(z^{\prime}),\quad T^{\prime 0z}=\gamma^{2}v\rho(z^{\prime}),\quad T^{\prime zz}=\gamma^{2}v^{2}\rho(z^{\prime}),\quad T^{\prime xx}=T^{\prime yy}=-\rho(z^{\prime}).

(5.4.18)

The wall is Lorentz contracted and
\sigma^{\prime}=\gamma\sigma
.
These last points will be relevant when we discuss the thermal friction in Section
7.5.1
.

Further aspects on axion domain walls

In this subsection, we treat in more detail how we precisely connect the theory about domain walls with the cosmological axion domain walls.

We begin by considering a non-Abelian
SU(3)
group, which can be related to the SM gluon field for the QCD axion or a dark gluon sector for ALPs.

We have a
U_{\text{PQ}}(1)
symmetry anomalous under
SU(3)
, whose pseudo-Nambu-Goldstone boson is the axion of our interest.
Then, the Lagrangian of the axion contains the following interaction term

\mathcal{L}_{a}=\frac{g_{s}}{8\pi}\frac{N_{\text{DW}}}{\eta}a\,G_{\mu\nu}^{b}\,\tilde{G}^{\mu\nu}_{b}

(5.4.19)

g_{s}
is, as before, the gauge coupling constant,
G_{\mu\nu}^{b}
the gauge boson field strength, and
\eta
is the
U_{\text{PQ}}(1)
symmetry-breaking VEV, related to the axion decay constant by

f_{a}=\frac{\eta}{N_{\text{DW}}},

(5.4.20)

For
N_{DW}=1
, the vacuum manifold for the ALP effective potential induced by
SU(3)
gauge interaction is trivial since it contains only identified minima as
a=0
and
a=2\pi f_{a}=2\pi\eta
, and then it is not topologically stable for Derrick’s theorem
[
101
]
.
However, for
N_{DW}>1
, the vacuum is composed of disconnected points corresponding to the discrete
Z_{N_{DW}}
symmetry, and the theory admits domain wall solutions that interpolate between neighbouring minima, as we show with an intuitive picture in Fig.
5.6
.

Figure 5.6:
Plot of the instanton potential
V(a)=\frac{\eta^{2}m_{a}^{2}}{N_{\rm DW}^{2}}\Big[1-\cos{\Big(N_{\rm DW}\frac{a}{\eta}\Big)}\Big]
in normalized units and with various values of the domain wall number
N_{\rm DW}
.

As done in the former literature
[
235
,
158
,
146
,
130
,
148
,
245
,
137
,
217
]
, and since we are interested in the dynamics after the creation of domain walls, at temperatures below the QCD phase transition
T_{QCD}
, we first capture the fundamental features of the axion potential by adopting the instanton potential, as before.

The simple instanton form of the potential is helpful since it allows us to obtain simple analytical solutions of domain walls with uniform velocity
\vec{v}

a(z)=f_{a}\Big[2\pi k+4\arctan{\Big[e^{\gamma m_a \hat{n} \cdot(\vec{x}+ \vec{v} t)}\Big]\Big\}},\qquad k=0,...,N_{\text{DW}}-1

(5.4.21)

where
\gamma=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-v^{2}}}
is the corresponding Lorentz factor of the velocity
v
and
\hat{n}
is the direction of motion normal to the wall worldsheet.
The typical width of a DW moving with velocity
\vec{v}
is then
\delta_{\rm DW}\sim(\gamma m_{a})^{-1}

with tension at rest

\sigma_{\text{DW}}=8m^{2}_{a}f_{a}.

(5.4.22)

Figure 5.7:

Schematic illustration of the fundamental features of axion cosmic strings and domain walls.
The cosmic string (left) is characterized by the axion field
\theta=a/\eta
, which undergoes an excursion of
2\pi
when rotating around the string.
The domain wall (right), attached to two strings with
N_{\rm DW}=2
, is associated with the axion field
\theta=a/f_{a}
, which also varies by
2\pi
across the wall.
Figure adapted from the analogous illustration in Ref.
[
206
]
.

After the formation of domain walls and according to numerical simulations, the energy density of the resulting string-wall network is soon dominated by the walls and, if
N_{\text{DW}}>1
, the DW network is stable and reach the scaling regime where the energy density of the DW network redshifts as
\rho_{DW}\sim\sigma_{DW}H
. This regime corresponds to having an order of one domain wall per Hubble sphere and a mildly relativistic
4
4

4

Tipical values of rms velocities
v
can be 0.5 or higher
[
189
]
.
regime for DWs
[
146
,
130
,
204
,
23
,
183
]
. Differently, for
N_{\text{DW}}=1
the DW network is unstable and decays soon
[
237
]
.

A DW network in a scaling regime would eventually dominate the energy density of the Universe, in contrast to cosmological observations.
Since the Universe is radiation-dominated in the epoch of interest, the temperature of radiation-DW equality can be roughly obtained by equating
\rho_{DW}
with
\rho_{\rm rad}\sim 3H^{2}M^{2}_{\text{Pl}}
.

Adopting the Hubble parameter
H
expression in terms of plasma temperature
T
, the following dominance temperature
T_{\text{dom}}
is obtained
[
52
]

T_{\text{dom}}\simeq 14\,\mathrm{MeV}\Bigg(\frac{\sigma_{\text{DW}}}{100\,\mathrm{TeV}}\Bigg)^{3/2}\,\Big(\frac{g_{*}}{10}\Big)^{-1/4},

(5.4.23)

where it is used
\rho_{DW}=2\sigma_{DW}\mathcal{A}\,H
with
\mathcal{A}=0.8
from numerical DWs simulations .
To collapse the DW network before dominating, we need a mechanism that annihilates the network at a temperature
T_{*}>T_{\text{dom}}
.

This is a requirement to determine whether the thermal friction, or a bias potential, is sufficient to solve the DW problem for a specific point in the axion parameter space.
Furthermore, a convenient quantity for the observations is
\alpha_{*}
, the DW energy normalised to the total energy

\alpha_{*}=\frac{\rho_{DW}}{\rho_{tot}}\simeq\frac{\rho_{DW}}{\rho_{r}}\simeq 0.02\,\Big(\frac{\sigma_{\text{DW}}}{100\mathrm{TeV}}\Big)^{3}\Big(\frac{T_{*}}{100\mathrm{MeV}}\Big)^{-2}\Big(\frac{g_{*}}{10}\Big)^{-1/2}

(5.4.24)

Another aspect for which the annihilation temperature
T_{*}
can also be relevant for cosmological observation is that, as the numerical simulations have proven
[
137
,
41
]
, the DW network in the scaling regime generates a large Stochastic Gravitational Wave Background (SGWB)
[
153
]
.

The expected density parameter
\Omega_{\text{gw}}(f)
is with a broken power law in frequency
f
and the signal is dominated by the last moment of emission, so it depends explicitly on
T_{*}
.
The density parameter redshifted to today is
[
40
]

\Omega_{\text{gw}}(f,T_{*})=\Omega_{\text{peak}}\times\begin{cases}\Big(\frac{f}{f_{\text{peak}}}\Big)^{3}\qquad&\text{if}\quad f\leq f_{\text{peak}}\\
\Big(\frac{f}{f_{\text{peak}}}\Big)^{-1}\qquad&\text{if}\quad f>f_{\text{peak}}\end{cases}

(5.4.25)

with

\displaystyle\Omega_{\text{peak}}\simeq 1.64\times 10^{-6}\times\Bigg(\frac{\tilde{\epsilon}}{0.7}\Bigg)\Bigg(\frac{\mathcal{A}}{0.8}\Bigg)^{2}\Bigg(\frac{g_{*}}{10}\Bigg)\Bigg(\frac{g_{s}}{10}\Bigg)^{-4/3}\times\Bigg(\frac{T_{\text{dom}}}{T_{*}}\Bigg)^{4}

(5.4.26)

and

f_{\text{peak}}\simeq 1.15\times 10^{-9}\,\mathrm{Hz}\times\Bigg(\frac{g_{*}}{10}\Bigg)^{1/2}\Bigg(\frac{g_{s}}{10}\Bigg)^{-1/3}\Bigg(\frac{T_{*}}{10\,\mathrm{MeV}}\Bigg)

(5.4.27)

Another interesting example of SGWB from axions comes from the Axion U(1) inflation
[
95
]
,which can lead to an amplification of gravitational waves
that could be detectable by Advanced LIGO or Advanced Virgo in the next few years.

Velocity-One scale model for domain walls

The idea of analytical models for topological defects networks stands on the idea of avoiding the difficulties coming from numerical simulations by evaluating the evolution dynamics of average properties of the network. In particular, in Cosmology one can be interested in
the mean energy density
\rho
and the root-mean-squared (rms) velocity
v
.

We outline in this subsection the approach by Ref.
[
189
]
for the Velocity-One Scale (VOS) model, since it will be helpful in order to clarify the approach we will adopt in Section
9
.

Figure 5.8:
Pic of a subhorizon domain wall inside the Hubble sphere centered in its center of mass.

Let us consider a subhorizon open domain wall as in Fig
5.8
, inside the Hubble horizon.

If we parametrise the wall surface
\mathcal{M}
by
x^{\mu}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
, inside a classical 4-dimensional spacetime with a metric
g_{\mu\nu}
and fix it to be the FLRW metric, the wall evolution is described by the 4-vector
x^{\mu}(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})=x^{\mu}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\tau)
where we have identified
\sigma_{0}=\tau
, where
\tau
is a real number parameter.

If the function
x^{\mu}
is smooth, we can parameterise the wall surface such that two tangential vectors will be orthogonal

\partial_{\sigma_{1}}x^{\mu}\,\partial_{\sigma_{2}}x_{\mu}=0

(5.4.28)

and the velocity of the wall
\dot{x}^{\mu}
to be only normal to the tangent surface
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{M}}
.
We use the minimal worldvolume Dirac action for the domain wall

\mathcal{S}=-\sigma_{DW}\int\sqrt{\gamma}\,\,d^{3}\sigma,

(5.4.29)

where
\sigma_{DW}
is a constant surface energy density, the induced metric is
\gamma_{ab}=g_{\mu\nu}\,x^{\mu}_{,a}\,x^{\nu}_{b}
with the determinant
\gamma=\frac{1}{6}\epsilon^{ab}\epsilon^{cd}\gamma_{ac}\gamma_{bd}
and where
\epsilon^{ab}
is the 2D Levi-Civita symbol.

We can easily obtain the EoMs for a domain wall by differentiating the Lagrangian density
\mathcal{L}=\sigma_{DW}\sqrt{\gamma}

d\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\gamma}\gamma^{ab}d\gamma_{ab},

(5.4.30)

and then obtain the Euler-Lagrange equations

\frac{\dot{a}}{a}\delta_{0\lambda}\sqrt{\gamma}\gamma^{ab}\gamma_{ab}-\partial_{c}\left(\sqrt{\gamma}\gamma^{ab}g_{\mu\lambda}\,x^{\mu}_{,a}\,\delta^{c}_{b}\right)=0.

(5.4.31)

Redefining the coordinates
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\rightarrow(s_{1},s_{2})
such that
|\frac{\partial x^{i}}{\partial s_{\alpha}}|^{2}=1
, with
\alpha=1,2
, we can introduce an orthonormal basis with
\zeta^{i}_{\alpha}=\frac{\partial x^{i}}{\partial s_{\alpha}}
and
n^{i}=\frac{\dot{x}^{i}}{|\dot{x}^{i}|}
.

This allows us to simplify Eq. (
5.4.31
) and write its zeroth component
(\lambda=0)
as

\dot{\varepsilon}+3\frac{\dot{a}}{a}\varepsilon\dot{x}^{i}\dot{x}_{i}=0,

(5.4.32)

and the spatial part
(\lambda=i)
contracted with the normal vector
n_{i}

\ddot{x}^{i}n_{i}+3\frac{\dot{a}}{a}\dot{x}^{i}n_{i}(1-\dot{x}^{i}\dot{x}_{i})=(1-\dot{x}^{i}\dot{x}_{i})k^{i}_{1}n_{i}+(1-\dot{x}^{i}\dot{x}_{i})k^{i}_{2}n_{i},

(5.4.33)

with
k_{\alpha}^{i}=\frac{\partial\zeta_{\alpha}^{i}}{\partial s_{\alpha}}
.

We note that the operations of the scalar products
k_{\alpha}^{i}n_{i}
are projections of the curvatures corresponding to
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
along the normal vector
n^{i}
. Consequently, we can write
k_{\alpha}^{i}=\frac{a}{R_{\alpha}}u_{\alpha}^{i}
where
u_{\alpha}^{i}
are unit vectors and
R_{\alpha}
are the relative curvature radii.

Averaging equations (
5.4.32
) and (
5.4.33
) over a network of subhorizon walls yields the velocity-one-scale equations for the mean energy density
\rho
and the rms velocity
v
:

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}

\displaystyle=-H\rho(1+3v^{2})-\frac{c_{w}\rho}{L}\,v,

(5.4.34)

\displaystyle\dot{v}

\displaystyle=(1-v^{2})\left[\frac{K}{L}-3Hv\right],

(5.4.35)

where we have introduced three macroscopic quantities: the averaged energy density
\rho

\rho=\frac{E}{V}=\frac{\sigma_{DW}a^{2}}{V}\int\varepsilon\,\,d^{2}\sigma,

(5.4.36)

the root-mean-squared (rms) velocity
v

v^{2}=\frac{\dot{x}^{2}\int\varepsilon\,\,d^{2}\sigma}{\int\varepsilon\,\,d^{2}\sigma},

(5.4.37)

and the correlation length
L=\sigma_{\rm DW}/\rho
, which gives a characteristic length scale of the network.

The equations can be rewritten in simpler terms for the correlation length and
v
as

\displaystyle\dot{L}

\displaystyle=H\rho\,(1+3v^{2})-c_{w}v,

(5.4.38)

\displaystyle\dot{v}

\displaystyle=(1-v^{2})\Bigg[\frac{K}{L}-3Hv\Bigg],

displaying the scaling fixed point with
L\propto t
and constant
v
, both in a radiation-dominated and matter-dominated universe.

We finally observe that the same Ref.
[
189
]
observes that such equations cannot fit properly the numerical simulations and we need to consider two energy-loss mechanisms which we insert phenomenologically and are inspired from analogous terms from string networks
[
248
,
132
]
:

•

Chopping mechanism with a term
\frac{c_{w}\rho}{L}\,v
, it takes care of the energy loss due to possible intersections of domain walls and creation of sphere-like objects that collapse

•

Scalar radiation term
d\,[k_{0}-k(v)]^{r}
produced by zero-mode perturbations on the wall surface.

Then it can be obtained from these additional energy-loss mechanisms, although not directly justified from a microscopic theory, the following equations

\displaystyle\dot{L}

\displaystyle=HL(1+3v^{2})+c_{w}v+d\,[k_{0}-k(v)]^{r},

(5.4.39)

\displaystyle\dot{v}

\displaystyle=(1-v^{2})\left[\frac{k(v)}{L}-3Hv\right].

(5.4.40)

The dimensionless momentum parameter
k(v)
was obtained to be

k(v)=k_{0}\,\frac{1-(qv^{2})^{\beta}}{1+(qv^{2})^{\beta}}\,,

(5.4.41)

where
\beta
and
q
are other unknown parameters obtained by fitting the VOS model with the numerical simulations and its analytical form is suggested from the expected behaviour in the non-relativistic and ultrarelativistic limit.
The fitted values from numerical simulations for the DW network without friction effects are, in the treatment of Ref.
[
189
]
:

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle=0.28\pm 0.01,\qquad r=1.30\pm 0.02,

(5.4.42a)

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=1.69\pm 0.08,\qquad k_{0}=1.73\pm 0.01,

(5.4.42b)

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle=4.27\pm 0.10,\qquad c_{w}=0.00\pm 0.01.

(5.4.42c)

Ref.
[
51
]
added another possible aspect, more relevant for high-mass ALPS, which is thermal friction, a subject of Sections
5.4.3

and Chapter
8
.
The basic idea, following the analogous one for cosmic strings in Ref.
[
248
]
, is to consider the possible relativistic expression for the 4-force acting on a domain wall from the primordial plasma and they obtain an effective contribution to the Hubble friction terms which is of the form
H+\frac{1}{l_{f}}
, where
l_{f}
is the friction length and can be interpreted as a mean free path.

In the last section of the thesis, we will indeed start to extend the results from the VOS model with a new idea of analytical model.

Chapter 6
Axion Electrodynamics

In this chapter, we outline in Subsection
6.1
the basic aspects of Axion Electrodynamics, in particular the Lagrangian of the theory, the resulting axion-modified Maxwell equations and we discuss their properties, in particular the interpretation of the axion 4-current as polarization and magnetization currents and, also, the connection of this formalism to Condensed Matter Physics.

6.1
Basic elements of Axion Electrodynamics

6.1.1
Lagrangian density of Axion Electrodynamics

We consider a pseudoscalar axion field present in our laboratory apparatus, which has a two-photon interaction with the electromagnetic field, as previously discussed in Section
3.2
.
The total Lagrangian density
\mathcal{L}
describing the interaction between the electromagnetic field and the axion field in a Minkowski space inside a linear dielectric and magnetic material with dielectric permittivity
\varepsilon
and magnetic permeability
\mu
is
[
65
,
238
,
237
,
147
,
193
]
:

\mathcal{L}=-\frac{1}{4}F^{\alpha\beta}H_{\alpha\beta}+\mathcal{L}_{a}-J^{\mu}A_{\mu}+\frac{1}{4}g_{a\gamma\gamma}a(x)F_{\mu\nu}\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu},

(6.1.1)

where
\tilde{F}^{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}F_{\gamma\delta}
is the dual Faraday tensor ,
\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
is the totally antisymmetric symbol with
\epsilon^{0123}=1
, and
m_{a}
is the mass of the axion. The tensor
H
is:

H_{\alpha\beta}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-D_{x}&-D_{y}&-D_{z}\\
D_{x}&0&H_{z}&-H_{y}\\
D_{y}&-H_{z}&0&H_{x}\\
D_{z}&H_{y}&-H_{x}&0\\
\end{pmatrix},

(6.1.2)

where
\vec{D}
and
\vec{H}
are the usual displacement electric and magnetic fields. They are related for a linear dielectric and magnetic material in its rest frame to electric
\vec{E}
and induction
\vec{B}
fields by
\vec{D}=\varepsilon\vec{E}
and
\vec{B}=\mu\vec{H}
.
J^{\mu}
is an external classical electrical 4-current.

We also define the dimensionless fields
\Theta(x)=g_{a\gamma\gamma}a(x)
and
\theta(x)=a(x)/f_{a}
.

6.1.2
Axion-Modified Maxwell equations

The interaction term in Eq. (
6.1.1
) can be written as an interaction Lagrangian density of the form
-J^{\nu}A_{\nu}
by taking

J^{\nu}=g_{a\gamma\gamma}\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}a=\frac{1}{2}g_{a\gamma\gamma}\partial_{\mu}a\;\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}A_{\sigma}.

(6.1.3)

The axions then generate an effective electromagnetic 4-current, which is thus
J_{a}=(\rho_{a},\vec{J}_{a})
, where

\begin{split}\rho_{a}&=g_{a\gamma\gamma}\vec{B}\cdot\nabla a,\\
\vec{J}_{a}&=g_{a\gamma\gamma}\nabla a\wedge\vec{E}-g_{a\gamma\gamma}\dot{a}\vec{B},\end{split}

(6.1.4)

and the continuity equation
\partial_{\mu}j_{a}^{\mu}=0
is simply a topological conservation law
\dot{\rho}_{a}+\nabla\cdot\vec{J}_{a}=0
.

Another equivalent way to see these axion dielectric properties is by observing from the structure of Eq. (
6.1.1
) that it is possible to redefine the tensor
H
as:

H^{a}_{\mu\nu}=H_{\mu\nu}-g_{a\gamma\gamma}\,a(x)\,\tilde{F}_{\mu\nu},

(6.1.5)

in order to rewrite Eq. (
6.1.1
) with the interaction term
\frac{1}{4}g_{a\gamma\gamma}a\tilde{F}_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}
included inside the ’free term’
-\frac{1}{4}F^{\mu\nu}H_{\mu\nu}
.
We then find that we can use different constitutive relations for
\vec{D}
and
\vec{H}
in Axion Electrodynamics:

\displaystyle\vec{D}_{a}=\varepsilon\vec{E}+\vec{P}_{a},

(6.1.6a)

\displaystyle\vec{B}=\mu\vec{H}_{a}+\vec{M}_{a},

(6.1.6b)

with
\vec{P}_{a}=-g_{a\gamma\gamma}a\vec{B}
the axion-induced polarization vector and
\vec{M}_{a}=-\mu\,g_{a\gamma\gamma}a\vec{E}
its magnetization.

We can then describe the charge and current densities (
6.1.4
) as polarization and magnetization currents
\rho_{a}=-\nabla\cdot\vec{P}_{a}
and
\vec{J}_{a}=\nabla\wedge\vec{M}_{a}+\dot{P}_{a}
, analogously to electrodynamics in gravitational backgrounds
[
177
]
.

The Euler-Lagrange equations associated with the Lagrangian density (
6.1.1
) are

\displaystyle\ddot{a}-\nabla^{2}a+V^{\prime}(a)+g_{a\gamma\gamma}\vec{E}\cdot\vec{B}

\displaystyle=0,

(6.1.7a)

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\vec{E}=\rho+g_{a\gamma\gamma}\vec{B}\cdot\nabla a,

(6.1.7b)

\displaystyle\nabla\wedge\vec{B}=\vec{J}+\dot{\vec{E}}-g_{a\gamma\gamma}\dot{a}\vec{B}+g_{a\gamma\gamma}\nabla a\wedge\vec{E},

(6.1.7c)

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\vec{B}=0,

(6.1.7d)

\displaystyle\nabla\wedge\vec{E}=-\dot{\vec{B}}.

(6.1.7e)

Equation (
6.1.7a
) is the classical equation of motion for the axion field interacting with the electromagnetic field. In contrast, equations (
6.1.7b
) and (
6.1.7c
) are the modified Maxwell equations with sources and the equations (
6.1.7d
) and (
6.1.7e
) are the "constraint Maxwell equations", that are unchanged since we assume the Bianchi identities
\partial_{\mu}\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu}=0
to be valid.

The fields in (
6.1.6a
) satisfy the following forms of dielectric Maxwell equations:

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\vec{D}_{a}

\displaystyle=\rho,

(6.1.8a)

\displaystyle\nabla\wedge\vec{H}_{a}

\displaystyle=\vec{J}+\dot{\vec{D}}_{a},

(6.1.8b)

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\vec{B}

\displaystyle=0,

(6.1.8c)

\displaystyle\nabla\wedge\vec{E}

\displaystyle=-\dot{\vec{B}},

(6.1.8d)

in accordance with our interpretation of the axion currents.

6.1.3
Electromagnetic 4-potential

As in classical electrodynamics, the differential equations for the 4-potential
A_{\mu}(x)=(\phi,\vec{A})
depend on the choice of gauge.
We can, in general, write the electric and magnetic fields as

\vec{E}=-\nabla\phi-\frac{\partial\vec{A}}{\partial t},\qquad\vec{B}=\nabla\wedge\vec{A}.

(6.1.9)

Consequently, we have in the vacuum:

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\Phi+\partial_{t}\left(\nabla\cdot\vec{A}\right)

\displaystyle=-\rho_{a},

(6.1.10)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}^{2}-\nabla^{2})\vec{A}+\nabla\left(\nabla\cdot\vec{A}+\partial_{t}\Phi\right)

\displaystyle=\vec{J}_{a}.

(6.1.11)

We will adopt the following gauge choice, namely
the Coulomb gauge
\nabla\cdot\vec{A}=0
(radiation gauge if
\rho_{a}=0
)
and the temporal gauge

A_{0}=\Phi=0.

In the case of the temporal gauge, we have the following wave equation:

\Box\vec{A}+\nabla(\nabla\cdot\vec{A})=-\dot{\Theta}\;\nabla\wedge\vec{A}-\nabla\Theta\wedge\frac{\partial\vec{A}}{\partial t}.

(6.1.12)

In the case of the radiation gauge with a time-dependent axion field, we obtain the equations

(\varepsilon\mu\partial_{t}^{2}-\nabla^{2})\vec{A}=-\mu\dot{\Theta}\;\nabla\wedge\vec{A}.

(6.1.13)

We can also derive the wave equations for the electric and magnetic fields

\nabla^{2}{\bf E}-\varepsilon\mu\ddot{\bf E}={\bf\nabla(\nabla\cdot E)}+\mu\dot{\bf J}+\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left[\dot{\Theta}{\bf B}+{\bf\nabla}\Theta{\bf\times E}\right],

(6.1.14)

\nabla^{2}{\bf H}-\varepsilon\mu\ddot{\bf H}=-{\bf\nabla\times J}-{\bf\nabla\times}[\dot{\Theta}{\bf B}+{\bf\nabla}\Theta{\bf\times E}].

(6.1.15)

The wave equations above are complicated in the sense that they contain the second-order derivatives of
\theta
. These may be conveniently removed if we consider the approximations with which we work out in Chapter
7
, by assuming constant axion derivatives. We will discuss the motivations of the utility of such an approximation in Subsections
7.3
and
7.4
.

6.1.4
Energy-momentum balance with a classical axion background

It is then easy to notice from the Lagrangian (
6.1.1
) that the total energy-momentum balance reads (with
J^{\mu}=0
):

\partial_{\mu}T^{\mu\nu}=0,

(6.1.16)

where
T^{\mu\nu}
is simply the sum of the free Minkowski stress-energy tensor
T^{M\mu\nu}=-\frac{1}{4}\eta^{\mu\nu}F_{\alpha\beta}H^{\alpha\beta}+F^{\mu}_{\rho}H^{\nu\rho}
and the free axion one
T^{\mu\nu}_{a}=\partial^{\mu}a\,\partial^{\nu}a-\eta^{\mu\nu}(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\rho}a\,\partial^{\rho}a-\frac{1}{2}m_{a}^{2}a^{2})
, since the coupling between the axion and photons is topological, as already discussed in Section
3.2
. If we consider the electromagnetic field alone, one gets, according to Ref.
[
65
]
:

\partial_{\nu}T^{M\nu}_{\mu}=-f^{M}_{\mu},

(6.1.17)

where
f^{M}_{\mu}=(f^{M}_{0},\vec{f}^{M})
whose spatial components are the components of Abraham’s force density
\vec{f}^{A}=(\varepsilon\mu-1)\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Big(\vec{E}\wedge\vec{H}\Big)-(\vec{E}\cdot\vec{B})\nabla\Theta

and
f^{M}_{0}=-g_{a\gamma\gamma}\dot{a}\vec{E}\cdot\vec{B}
.

6.1.5
Axion Electrodynamics in Condensed Matter

A Weyl semimetal constitutes an intriguing phase of topological quantum matter, with fascinating physical properties, including protected surface states and a unique electromagnetic response, which is useful for practical applications, thus giving our theoretical results a broader interest, since our formalism can be adopted for the electromagnetic properties of such materials.
It is already subject of extensive literature (see e.g. Refs.
[
201
,
262
]
), the electromagnetic properties of a Weyl semimetal can be described by a Chern-Simons theory with Lagrangian (
6.1.1
), where the effective axion field is

\Theta(x)=b_{0}t-\vec{b}\cdot\vec{r}=b_{\mu}x^{\mu}.

(6.1.18)

The quantities
b_{\mu}
are of relevant physical meaning for the material, since they are related to the energy shift
b_{0}
and the momentum shift
\vec{b}
of the specific Weyl point of the material, whose Hamiltonian is

h_{W}(\vec{k})=b_{0}+v\vec{\sigma}\cdot(\vec{k}-\vec{b}).

(6.1.19)

In the following, we will consider applications where the derivatives of the axion field are constant and uniform, which can be applied for the physical case of
b_{0}
and
\vec{b}
, where they are really constant.
It is then interesting to know the expected orders of magnitude for Weyl semimetals.

Typical orders for the 4-momentum shifts of the Weyl point are
b_{0}\sim 10^{-1}\,$\text{\,}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}$
and
|\vec{b}|\sim 10\,$\text{\,}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}$
as mentioned in Ref.
[
262
]
.
These values are typically higher than the high-energy physics case, helping significantly on the possible applications which we develop in the following sections, in particular for the Casimir effect between two plates and the system we treat in Section
7.4.4
.

.

Part II
Results and applications

Chapter 7
Deeper aspects of Axion Electrodynamics

7.1
Introduction

Starting from the theory of Axion Electrodynamics that we have outlined in Chapter
6
, we calculate the axionic modifications to the electromagnetic Casimir energy using the Green’s function method. This will be done in both cases of the axion field initially assumed to be purely time-dependent and when the axion field configuration is a static domain wall, which we take to be strictly space-dependent.

For the first case, it means that the oscillating axion background is assumed to resemble a dark matter axion fluid at rest, approximated as cold, and neglecting the velocity dispersion of the input dark matter axions, in a conventional ideal Casimir setup with two infinite parallel conducting plates.

In contrast, in the second case, we evaluate the radiation pressure acting on an axion domain wall. We extend previous theories to include finite temperatures.
This work is also related to earlier investigations in Refs.
[
237
,
152
,
51
,
4
,
77
,
238
,
142
]
.

This is an overview of this Chapter:

•

In Section
7.2
we develop the basics of the Green’s function method in Axion Electrodynamics and use it in practice for time-dependent axion backgrounds, namely with constant time derivative and oscillating behaviour in Section
7.3
. We calculate the Casimir force between two perfectly conducting parallel plates for the first case and provide estimates for the second one, as we did in Ref.
[
123
]
.

•

Section
7.4.3
compares our results with formerly known phenomena in Classical Electrodynamics, such as the Faraday effect and optical activity in chiral media, along with other works on the optical activity of Axion Electrodynamics. We discuss an application in Section
7.4.4
, which was the main object of our Ref.
[
64
]
.

•

In Sections
7.4
and
7.5
we treat space-dependent axion backgrounds. We discuss their optical properties and dispersion relations.

•

Section
7.5
, in particular in Subsection
7.5.1
, deals with the calculation of the electromagnetic radiation pressure acting on a planar axion domain wall. We use Casimir methods and connect them with former methods.
We discuss our results for applications in Axion Cosmology and Condensed Matter Physics, as we did in Ref.
[
123
]
.

The two classes of axion time-dependent backgrounds considered are also of experimental interest, for example, for observing the virialized axion dark matter of the Big Flow, the branch of cold dark matter halo of the Milky Way where the Earth is supposed to stay
[
238
]
.

This is seen in the long-wavelength approximation, i.e., when we assume the experimental setup to have a typical spatial dimension
L
much smaller than the de Broglie wavelength of the local axion field, so that we take
a(t)\simeq a_{0}\sin(\omega_a t)
.
For a dark matter QCD axion if we take
v\sim 10^{-3}
, which is the typical value of rms velocity in the Big Flow, and
\omega_{a}\sim m_{a}
, we have a typical value of

\lambda_{DB}\simeq 10^{-3}m_{a}^{-1}

(7.1.1)

We can take a further approximation of taking the time derivative of the axion field to be constant, which makes sense when the argument of the sine of the oscillatory axion can be taken much smaller than 1.

From a physical point of view, it can be done when the spatial dimensions of the system are much smaller than the Compton wavelength, and can be just a pedagogical first approximation for the axion. In that case, it is exact for the effective axion field in Weyl semimetals.

The axion field is treated here as a fixed background field, since this is true at first order in the perturbation theory where the back reaction of the electromagnetic field onto axions is neglected, partly analogous to what happens in the linear approximation for weak gravitational perturbations in General Relativity
[
170
]
. We have already discussed this last point in Section
6.1.4
.

7.2
Green’s functions

We first develop the Green’s function approach in Axion Electrodynamics, using the temporal gauge.
We start from Eq. (
6.1.12
) with
\varepsilon=\mu=1
and define the kernel
\vec{G}_{ij}(x,x^{\prime})
, such that:

A_{i}(x)=\int d^{4}x^{\prime}\;G_{ij}(x,x^{\prime})\,J^{j}(x^{\prime}).

(7.2.1)

Due to causality, the variable
t^{\prime}
is only integrated over
t^{\prime}\leq t
. From Eq. (
6.1.12
) we find

\begin{split}\Box G_{ij}(x,x^{\prime})+\partial_{i}(\partial_{k}G_{kj}(x,x^{\prime}))-\dot{\Theta}(x)\epsilon_{ikl}\partial_{k}G_{lj}(x,x^{\prime})-\epsilon_{ikl}\partial_{k}\Theta(x)\;\partial_{t}G_{lj}(x,x^{\prime})=\\
\delta_{ij}\delta^{(4)}(x^{\mu}-x^{\prime\mu}).\end{split}

(7.2.2)

The
G
kernel (
7.2.1
) is equal to
iD_{ij}(x^{\mu},y^{\mu})
, where
D
is the retarded Green’s function of the vector potential
\vec{A}
defined as

iD^{R}_{ij}(x^{\mu},y^{\mu})=\begin{cases}\langle{A_{i}(x)A_{j}(y)-A_{j}(x)A_{i}(y)}\rangle\;\;\;\textit{if}\;\;\;\;x^{0}-y^{0}>0,\\
0\;\;\;\;\rm{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(7.2.3)

It can be derived by the Schwinger-Dyson equations
[
232
]
for Axion Electrodynamics, which is exactly Eq. (
7.2.2
), when we take the temporal gauge.

As shown in Refs.
[
179
,
196
]
and
[
50
]
, the calculation of the retarded Green’s function (
7.2.3
) is useful to get the significant two-point physical averages between electric and magnetic fields.
One can easily find that with the above choice of gauge:

\displaystyle\langle{E_{i}(x)E_{j}(x^{\prime})}\rangle

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\partial_{t^{\prime}}\langle{A_{i}(x)A_{j}(x^{\prime})}\rangle,

(7.2.4a)

\displaystyle\langle{B_{i}(x)B_{j}(x^{\prime})}\rangle

\displaystyle=\curl_{il}\curl^{\prime}_{jm}\langle{A_{l}(x)A_{m}(x^{\prime})}\rangle,

(7.2.4b)

\displaystyle\langle{B_{i}(x)E_{j}(x^{\prime})}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\curl_{il}\partial_{t^{\prime}}\langle{A_{l}(x)A_{j}(x^{\prime})}\rangle,

(7.2.4c)

Similarly, this applies also to the other two-point functions. We will also use the Fourier transforms of the two-point functions, e.g.

\langle{A_{\alpha}(x)A_{\beta}(y)}\rangle_{\omega}=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}dt\,e^{i\omega t}\langle{A_{\alpha}(t,\vec{x})A_{\beta}(0,\vec{y})}\rangle,

(7.2.5)

from which the relations (
7.2.4
) can be used to find the corresponding ones for the two-point functions involving electric and magnetic fields.

If the Green’s function is a function of just the variables
t-t^{\prime},\,x-x^{\prime}\,\,\text{and}\,\,y-y^{\prime}
, we can define the
reduced Green’s function
g_{ij}(z,z^{\prime},\omega,\tilde{k})
:

G_{ij}(x,x^{\prime})=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d^{2}\tilde{k}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-i\omega(t-t^{\prime})}e^{i\tilde{k}\cdot(\vec{r}-\vec{r}^{\prime})}g_{ij}(z,z^{\prime},\omega,\tilde{k}),

(7.2.6)

with
\tilde{k}=(k_{x},k_{y})
. We also adopt the useful quantity
\kappa^{2}=\omega^{2}-k_{x}^{2}-k_{y}^{2}.

The utility of the reduced Green’s function is evident from the premise: it is advantageous in systems with translation symmetry along the
x-y
plane.

In the following, we will indeed focus on the Green’s function in the vacuum to calculate Casimir’s forces in such a symmetric system.
We can find straightforwardly from the reduced Green’s function, with
\varepsilon=\mu=1
:

\begin{split}\langle{T^{M}_{zz}}\rangle_{\omega,\tilde{k}}=\frac{1}{2i}\left[-\kappa^{2}g_{zz}+(\omega^{2}-k_{y}^{2})g_{xx}+(\omega^{2}-k_{x}^{2})g_{yy}+ik_{y}\left(\partial_{z}g_{yz}-\partial_{z}^{\prime}g_{zy}\right)\right.\\
\left.+ik_{x}\left(\partial_{z}g_{xz}-\partial_{z}^{\prime}g_{zx}\right)+k_{x}k_{y}(g_{xy}+g_{yx})+\partial_{z}\partial_{z}^{\prime}(g_{xx}+g_{yy})\right].\end{split}

(7.2.7)

For our purpose of evaluating zero-point energies, we will adopt the retarded Green’s function with
\mu\neq 1
to find the expression in the vacuum by using the fluctuation-dissipation theorem
[
178
]
:

\langle{A_{\alpha}(x)A_{\beta}(y)}\rangle_{\omega}=\frac{i}{2}\left[D^{R}_{\alpha\beta}(\omega,\vec{x},\vec{y})-D^{R*}_{\beta\alpha}(\omega,\vec{y},\vec{x})\right],

(7.2.8)

and then take the limit
\mu\rightarrow 1+i0
.

Notice that in the case of temporal gauge, the Green’s function can be treated as a
3\times 3
tensor, while this is not the case for other gauge choices, where we need to treat it fully as a
4\times 4
tensor.

If the axion field is only a function of time
t
, it is convenient to adopt the radiation gauge, and we will do it in the following section. We will instead adopt the temporal gauge for space-dependent axion fields from Section (
7.5
) on.

7.3
Purely time-dependent axion field

Assuming
\Theta(x)=\Theta(t)
and a homogenous medium with electrical permittivity
\epsilon=1
and magnetic permeability
\mu
, we obtain the following equation for the Green’s function
G_{kj}(x,y)
using Eq. (
6.1.13
)

\left[\delta_{ik}\Box(\mu)-\mu\,\dot{\Theta}(t)\,\epsilon_{lik}\nabla_{l}\right]G_{kj}(x,y)=\mu\delta_{ij}\delta^{(4)}(x^{\mu}-y^{\mu}),

(7.3.1)

where we have defined the operator
\Box(\mu)=\mu\,\partial_{t}^{2}-\nabla^{2}
.

The solution of Eq. (
7.3.1
) is generally dependent on
\vec{r}-\vec{r^{\prime}}
and
t-t^{\prime}
, but also explicitly on
t
, then it is not trivial to find solutions for any
\Theta(t)
. We will consequently limit ourselves to two specific cases of interest, i.e.
\Theta=\alpha_{0}t
and
\Theta=\Theta_{0}\sin(\omega_a t)
, as already discussed.

7.3.1
Case of constant axion time derivative
\alpha_{0}

If we consider
\dot{\Theta}=\alpha_{0}
as constant in time and uniform in space, we can take, similarly to
[
4
]
, the Fourier Transform of the Green’s function and obtain from Eq. (
7.3.1
) the following equation:

\left[\delta_{ik}(-\mu\omega^{2}+k^{2})+i\mu\alpha_{0}\epsilon_{lik}k_{l}\right]\tilde{G}_{kj}(\vec{k},\omega)=\mu\delta_{ij}.

(7.3.2)

To obtain the Green’s function in the vacuum, we perform the calculations with a magnetic permeability
\mu=1+i\tilde{\mu}(\omega)
, where
\tilde{\mu}(\omega)\ll 1
is first order, and evaluate the limit for
\tilde{\mu}\rightarrow 0
, as analogously done in Ref.
[
178
]
.
The usual approach in the literature is with an electrical permittivity
\varepsilon(\omega)=1+i\epsilon(\omega)
, to obtain the quantum mechanical result for the Green’s function.
However, there exist real media where magnetic viscosity is present (see e.g. Ref.
[
174
]
):

\mu=\Re{(\mu)}+i\,\epsilon\,\frac{\omega}{\omega_{0}}.

(7.3.3)

This is analogous to the case of electric permittivity, and we have the imaginary part
\Im{(\mu)}>0
when
\omega>0
. This justifies our choice of
\sgn(\omega)
in the following.

From Eq. (
7.3.2
) we get, similarly to Ref.
[
4
]
, the retarded Green’s function as the inverse operator of the expression in square brackets on the LHS of Eq. (
7.3.2
) in the limit of
\mu=1+i\tilde{\mu}\sgn(\omega)\rightarrow 1+i0\sgn(\omega)
:

\tilde{G}_{jk}(\vec{k},\omega)=(\omega^{2}-|\vec{k}|^{2})\tilde{A}(\omega,\vec{k},\vec{\beta})\delta_{jk}+i\tilde{A}(\omega,\vec{k},\vec{\beta})\epsilon_{jkl}\beta_{l}+\frac{1}{\omega^{2}-|\vec{k}|^{2}+i0\sgn(\omega)}\tilde{A}(\omega,\vec{k},\vec{\beta})\beta_{j}\beta_{k},

(7.3.4)

where
\vec{\beta}
is a 3-vector with components
\beta_{j}=\alpha_{0}k_{j}
, whose squared module is
\beta^{2}
and

\tilde{A}(\omega,\vec{k},\vec{\beta})=\frac{1}{(\omega^{2}-|\vec{k}|^{2})^{2}-\beta^{2}+i0\sgn(\omega)}.

(7.3.5)

The ordinary electrodynamics limits can be obtained by setting
\alpha_{0}=0
.
We can now use the Fourier transforms for evaluating the spectral energy density
\rho_{em}(\omega)
of the electromagnetic field at temperature
T
and the Casimir force between two parallel conducting plates in such a fixed axionic background.

Spectral energy density
\rho_{em}(\omega)
of the electromagnetic field

To get the zero-point spectral energy density, we calculate the inverse Fourier transform, which we call
\tilde{G}(\vec{r},\omega)
, of the Green’s function (
7.3.4
) in the position space and take the correlation functions in the limit
\vec{r}\rightarrow 0
.
The usual spectral energy density of the electromagnetic field is

\rho(\omega,\vec{r})\,d\omega=\frac{1}{2}\left[2\langle{\vec{E}^{2}(\vec{r})}\rangle_{\omega,T}+2\langle{\vec{B}^{2}(\vec{r})}\rangle_{\omega,T}\right]\frac{d\omega}{2\pi},

(7.3.6)

where the factors two inside the brackets are inserted because we follow the definition of spectral densities given in Ref.
[
178
]
: average spectral densities are defined as integrals in
\omega
from
-\infty
to
+\infty
.
We also exploit the distributional relation:

\frac{1}{x\pm i0}=\mathcal{P}{\frac{1}{x}}\mp i\pi\delta(x).

(7.3.7)

where
\mathcal{P}
is the principal part and
\delta
is the Dirac delta distribution
[
178
,
25
,
241
]
.
We expand the spectral energy density in a perturbative series:

\rho(\omega,\vec{r})=\rho^{(0)}(\omega,\vec{r})+\rho^{(1)}(\omega,\vec{r})+\rho^{(2)}(\omega,\vec{r})+\text{(higher-order terms).}

(7.3.8)

We can develop a perturbative expansion for the Green’s function in terms of the perturbative factor
\alpha_{0}
:

\tilde{G}_{jk}(\vec{k},\omega)\sim\tilde{G}^{(0)}_{jk}(\vec{k},\omega)+\tilde{G}^{(1)}_{jk}(\vec{k},\omega)+\tilde{G}^{(2)}_{jk}(\vec{k},\omega),

(7.3.9)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{G}^{(0)}_{jk}(\vec{k},\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\omega^{2}-k^{2}+i0\sgn{\omega}},

(7.3.10a)

\displaystyle\tilde{G}^{(1)}_{jk}(\vec{k},\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{(\omega^{2}-k^{2})^{2}+i0\sgn{\omega}}\epsilon_{jkl}\beta_{l}=\frac{i}{(\omega^{2}-k^{2})^{2}+i0\sgn{\omega}}\epsilon_{jkl}(\alpha_{0}k_{l}),

(7.3.10b)

\displaystyle\tilde{G}^{(2)}_{jk}(\vec{k},\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta^{2}}{(\omega^{2}-k^{2})^{3}+i0\sgn{\omega}}\left[\delta_{jk}+\frac{\epsilon_{jkl}\beta_{l}}{\omega^{2}-k^{2}}\right]+\frac{1}{(\omega^{2}-k^{2})^{3}+i0\sgn{\omega}}\alpha^{2}_{0}k_{j}k_{k}.

(7.3.10c)

Analogously to Ref.
[
178
]
, we can obtain the spectral density associated with the zero-order term
\rho^{(0)}(\omega)
by integrating over the domain of
\vec{k}

\rho^{(0)}(\omega)d\omega=d\omega\;\omega^{2}\left[\frac{1}{2}\omega+\frac{\omega}{e^{\frac{\omega}{T}}-1}\right].

(7.3.11)

This is the familiar result from QED: the zero-point energy of the electromagnetic field at a given temperature
T
is the sum of the vacuum zero-point energy at zero Kelvin temperature and the black-body radiation energy at the given temperature
T
.

The first order term
\rho^{(1)}(\omega)
is trivially zero, while we have a contribution of order
\mathcal{O}(g^{2}_{a\gamma\gamma})
:

\rho^{(2)}(\omega)\;d\omega=d\omega\;\omega^{2}\left[\frac{1}{2}\frac{\dot{\alpha_{0}}^{2}}{8\omega}+\frac{1}{e^{\frac{\omega}{T}}-1}\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{8\omega}\right].

(7.3.12)

We notice the frequency dependence of the spectral energy density in Eq. (
7.3.12
), from which we can find the spectral emissivity using Planck’s law:

•

The first term on the RHS is proportional to the frequency and can be physically interpreted as a blue noise in the frequency domain.

•

The second term is similar to the first one in terms of frequency dependence, but it has an additional Bose-Einstein temperature-dependent weight. For
T\gg\omega
it behaves as
\simeq T
, so it can be regarded as a flat noise, and is bigger than the zero-temperature term. For
T\ll\omega
, the Bose-Einstein weight would give approximately the Boltzmann weight
e^{-\frac{\omega}{T}}
, so an exponential noise.

Dispersion relations

Here, we briefly discuss the dispersion relations. The Green’s function (
7.3.4
) has poles, to which the dispersion relations are associated :

\omega_{\pm}=\sqrt{|\vec{k}|^{2}\pm\alpha_{0}|\vec{k}|}\qquad\omega_{0}=|\vec{k}|.

(7.3.13)

These dispersion relations can be interpreted physically as follows: consider the equations of motion of the vector potential for a solution propagating along the direction of the
z
-axis:

\displaystyle\begin{split}(-\omega^{2}+k_{z}^{2})\mathcal{A}_{x}&=\dot{\Theta}k_{z}\mathcal{A}_{y},\\
(-\omega^{2}+k_{z}^{2})\mathcal{A}_{y}&=-\dot{\Theta}k_{z}\mathcal{A}_{x},\\
(-\omega^{2}+k_{z}^{2})\mathcal{A}_{z}&=0.\end{split}

(7.3.14)

where
\mathcal{A}_{i}
are components of the Fourier transforms of the vector potential
\vec{A}
.
This assumption on the propagation direction does not limit generality, since we treat a system in the vacuum which is isotropic and it is always possible to find an inertial reference frame where an electromagnetic plane wave propagates along an assigned
z
-axis.

It is then easy to demonstrate the dispersion relation
\omega_{\pm}
of (
7.3.13
) for real transverse photons. Indeed, if we define the fields
\mathcal{A}_{\pm}=\mathcal{A}_{x}\pm i\mathcal{A}_{y}
, we obtain the dispersion relations
\omega_{\pm}
with
k_{x}=k_{y}=0
. However, for the full expression of
\omega_{\pm}
in Eq. (
7.3.13
) we need to use the relativistic invariance of the magnitude of the wave 4-vector.

Its physical meaning is as follows: a left-circular polarized wave has a different frequency than a right-circular one with the same
\vec{k}
and the optical angle rotates. This is due to the optical activity of the vacuum in Axion Electrodynamics as we shall discuss it in Section (
7.4.3
).

We also notice that a solution for the equation of
\mathcal{A}_{z}
, one with the dispersion relation
\omega=|\vec{k}|
and corresponding to longitudinal photons, is simply zero, so that the dispersion relation corresponds to virtual photons.

Casimir force between parallel plates

Here we evaluate the axion modifications to the usual Casimir force between two parallel perfectly conducting plates (see Refs.
[
196
,
232
]
for a treatment without an axion background).
Our result is comparable to that obtained by Ref.
[
169
]
, while improving upon their result.

L

Figure 7.1:
Scheme of a system of two metallic parallel plates, for which we measure the Casimir force per unit area. The controlled separation
L
is experimentally the metrological "lever arm". The z-axis is directed towards the high. We do not use a usual notation of
d
for the lever arm distance to not confuse it with the
d
we adopt for denoting the extended number of dimensions, which we need for dimensional regularization in the following.

We can evaluate the zero-point energy
u_{em}(L,\alpha_{0})
per unit transverse area by evaluating the following expression:

u_{em}(L,\dot{\Theta})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{n}\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\sqrt{|\vec{k}|^{2}+\frac{n^{2}\pi^{2}}{L^{2}}\pm\alpha_{0}\sqrt{|\vec{k}|^{2}+\frac{n^{2}\pi^{2}}{L^{2}}}}.

(7.3.15)

This can be expected by a Casimir approach, as given by the dispersion relations.

The expression (
7.3.27
) can be demonstrated by a Green’s function approach. We assume the
z
-axis to be the direction normal to the plates.
We need to solve the Green’s function equations with the proper boundary conditions. Since we assume to have two perfectly conducting plates, we have boundary conditions for the electric field and the magnetic field at
z=0,L
:

B_{z}=0\qquad\vec{E}_{\parallel}=0,

(7.3.16)

where
\vec{E}_{\parallel}
is the parallel component of the electric field, corresponding to the Green’s function:

\displaystyle g_{ij}(x-x^{\prime},y-y^{\prime},z,z^{\prime})|_{z=0,z=L}

\displaystyle=0\quad i\neq z,j\neq z,

(7.3.17a)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}g_{zz}(x-x^{\prime},y-y^{\prime},z,z^{\prime})|_{z=0,z=L}

\displaystyle=0.

(7.3.17b)

These conditions, along with the translational invariance along x and y directions, allow us to write the Green’s function in the following Fourier expansion for
0<z<L
and
0<z^{\prime}<L
:

\displaystyle G_{ij}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d^{2}\tilde{k}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{i\tilde{k}\cdot(\tilde{r}-\tilde{r}^{\prime})}\frac{2}{L}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sin{\left(\frac{n \pi}{L}z\right)}\sin{\left(\frac{n \pi}{L}z'\right)}\tilde{g}_{ij}(\tilde{k},n)\quad i\neq z,j\neq z,

(7.3.18a)

\displaystyle G_{zz}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d^{2}\tilde{k}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{i\tilde{k}\cdot(\tilde{r}-\tilde{r}^{\prime})}\frac{2}{L}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\iota(n)\cos{\left(\frac{n \pi}{L}z\right)}\cos{\left(\frac{n \pi}{L}z'\right)}\tilde{g}_{zz}(\tilde{k},n),

(7.3.18b)

where
\tilde{k}=(k_{x},k_{y})
,
\tilde{r}=(x,y)
and
\iota(n)=1
for
n>0
, while
\iota(n=0)=1/2
.

The reduced function
\tilde{G}(\tilde{k},n)
consequently satisfies the equations (
7.3.2
), with solution Eq. (
7.3.4
) ,where
k_{z}=\frac{n\pi}{L}
.
We now have two perspectives of looking at Eq. (
7.3.27
), which will allow us to calculate the Casimir force.

Energy density per transversal area

Here we adopt the form (
7.3.4
) for the Green’s function.
If we exploit the relation (
7.3.7
)
and the expression of the Green’s function (
7.3.4
) we get

\frac{1}{2}\langle{\vec{E}^{2}+\vec{B}^{2}}\rangle_{\omega,k_{x},k_{y},n}=\pi\frac{\omega}{2}\sum_{\pm}\left[\delta\left(\omega-\sqrt{|k|^{2}\pm\alpha_{0}|k|}\right)-\delta\left(\omega+\sqrt{|k|^{2}\pm\alpha_{0}|k|}\right)\right],

(7.3.19)

where
|k|^{2}=k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}+\left(\frac{n\pi}{L}\right)^{2}
.
We can then exploit the properties of the Dirac delta distribution by integrating in the
\frac{\omega}{2\pi}
domain from
-\infty
to
+\infty
, and then obtain

\frac{1}{2}\langle{\vec{E}^{2}+\vec{B}^{2}}\rangle_{k_{x},k_{y},n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\pm}\sqrt{k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}+\frac{n^{2}\pi^{2}}{L^{2}}\pm\alpha_{0}\sqrt{k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}+\frac{n^{2}\pi^{2}}{L^{2}}}}.

(7.3.20)

We also mention that
\langle{\vec{A}\cdot\vec{B}}\rangle_{k_{x},k_{y},n}
and
\langle{\vec{E}\cdot\vec{B}}\rangle_{k_{x},k_{y},n}
are trivially equal to zero. Consequently, we have
\langle{T^{00}_{em}}\rangle_{k_{x},k_{y},n}=\frac{1}{2}\langle{\vec{E}^{2}+\vec{B}^{2}}\rangle_{k_{x},k_{y},n}
, from which it is easy to get the energy per unit transverse area to be equal to the expression (
7.3.27
).

Casimir force from the reduced Green’s function

In general, the Casimir force is a physical force that exists between dielectric or metallic surfaces, resulting from quantum fluctuations of the electromagnetic field.
From a general perspective, it can be treated with different methods, but the essential ingredient is the difference between the normal Maxwell stress components
T_{zz}
on the two sides of a boundary, yielding a pressure on them.
This can be generally done with various geometries of the boundaries and the three main geometries are shown in Fig.
7.2
.

L
Sphere-plane (Casimir force)
L
Parallel plates (Casimir force)
Atom or BEC
L
Atom-surface (Casimir-Polder force)

Figure 7.2:
The three main geometries of the current experiments with Casimir setups.
The system with the parallel plates is conceptually clean,overall for theoretical calculations, but it is extremely hard to build experimentally. Plates need to be held parallel within angles
\theta\ll 1\,\mu
rad and furthermore electrostatic patches are problematic
[
99
,
100
]
.
In the case of the sphere-plane setup, e.g., the alignment is easy and performed in AFM-style force readout
[
198
,
83
]
. BEC stands for Bose-Einstein Condensate.

The Casimir force can be derived from equilibrium statistical thermodynamics, as an application of the fluctuation-dissipation theorem, which relates the two-point functions of the electromagnetic field to the imaginary part of retarded Green function, as in Ref.
[
178
]
and before, as well as using other approaches, for instance that in Ref.
[
66
]
. Alternatively, the effect can be seen as a manifestation of quantum field fluctuations
[
196
]
.

We evaluate the expression (
7.2.7
) using the equation (
7.3.2
), when
\mu=1
. It is then easy to obtain by means of the expression (
7.3.18
) the following expression

\langle{T_{zz}}\rangle_{\omega,k_{x},k_{y}}|_{z=z^{\prime}=0,L}=-2i\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\frac{n^{2}\pi^{2}}{L^{3}}\frac{\kappa^{2}-\frac{n^{2}\pi^{2}}{L^{2}}}{(\kappa^{2}-\frac{n^{2}\pi^{2}}{L^{2}})^{2}-\alpha^{2}_{0}(k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}+\frac{n^{2}\pi^{2}}{L^{2}})},

(7.3.21)

where
\kappa^{2}=\omega^{2}-k_{x}^{2}-k_{y}^{2}
.

This series can be treated by noticing that each
n
-th term of the series can be written as a sum of two
\pm
terms, as we have shown in Ref.
[
123
]
, by performing a standard complex frequency rotation
\omega\rightarrow\zeta=i\omega
, similarly to Refs.
[
196
]
, and integrating over
\zeta/{2\pi}
. We then obtain

\langle{T_{zz}}\rangle_{k_{x},k_{y}}|_{z=z^{\prime}=0,L}=\sum_{\pm}\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\frac{n^{2}\pi^{2}}{L^{3}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}+\frac{n^{2}\pi^{2}}{L^{2}}\pm\alpha_{0}\sqrt{k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}+\frac{n^{2}\pi^{2}}{L^{2}}}}}.

(7.3.22)

It is easy to see from Eqs. (
7.3.21
) and (
7.3.22
) that, up to contact terms, we are dealing with divergent expressions. This is because the physical quantity we observe is the discontinuity of
T_{zz}
, not the single values on the two sides of the interface (which is
z=0^{+}
in this case).
We also need to calculate
T_{zz}
at
z=0^{-}
.

We need to solve Eq. (
7.3.1
) with
z<0
and
z^{\prime}<0
(the case with
z>L
gives the same results for symmetry) with the boundary conditions (
7.3.17
) at
z=0
and
g\sim e^{-ikz}
for
z\rightarrow-\infty
. This can be done similarly to what done in Stakgold’s book
[
241
]
, by observing that, due to the boundary condition at
z=0
, the sine and cosine Fourier transforms satisfy Eq. (
7.3.2
).

We can then solve the equations and obtain the following expression:

\langle{T_{zz}}\rangle_{\omega,k_{x},k_{y}}|_{z=z^{\prime}=0^{-}}=-2i\int_{0}^{+\infty}k_{z}^{2}\frac{\kappa^{2}-k_{z}^{2}}{(\kappa^{2}-k_{z}^{2})^{2}-\alpha_{0}^{2}(k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}+k_{z}^{2})}dk_{z}.

(7.3.23)

We similarly manipulate this expression as for the one at
0^{+}
and omit contact terms. We obtain the Casimir force per unit area

f(L,\alpha_{0})=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{dk_{x}}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{dk_{y}}{2\pi}\frac{1}{L}\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\sum_{\pm}\left[\frac{\frac{n^{2}\pi^{2}}{L^{2}}\pm\alpha_{0}\frac{n^{2}\pi^{2}}{L^{2}\sqrt{k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}+\frac{n^{2}\pi^{2}}{L^{2}}}}}{\sqrt{|\vec{k}|^{2}+n^{2}\pi^{2}/L^{2}\pm\alpha_{0}\sqrt{|\vec{k}|^{2}+\frac{n^{2}\pi^{2}}{L^{2}}}}}\right].

(7.3.24)

It is easy to notice that the following formal relation between Casimir pressure and electromagnetic energy density holds:

f(L,\alpha_{0})=-\frac{\partial u_{em}(L,\alpha_{0})}{\partial L},

(7.3.25)

confirming our results.
This result can be understood by means of the Thermodynamics’ Second Law

dU=T\,dS-p\,dV=-p\,dV
when the temperature is zero. We observe this since it is in general not valid at temperature
T
, since entropy can change, but we can calculate the pressure as
p=-\Big(\frac{\partial U}{\partial V}\Big)_{T,S}
.

To evaluate the Casimir force deriving from the expression of the pressure (
7.3.27
), it is convenient to adopt the zeta function regularization method and extend it to a dimension
d\neq 2
(see e.g. Milton
[
196
]
and Brevik
[
67
]
).

We employ the Schwinger proper-time representation for the square root
[
232
]

u_{em}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\pm}\sum_{n}\int\frac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{dt}{t}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,e^{-t\left(k^{2}+n^{2}\pi^{2}/L^{2}\pm\alpha_{0}\sqrt{|\vec{k}|^{2}+\frac{n^{2}\pi^{2}}{L^{2}}}\right)}\frac{1}{\Gamma(-\frac{1}{2})},

(7.3.26)

It is not easy to evaluate it exactly, so we will calculate it perturbatively up to second order in
\alpha_{0}
.
It is possible to write the Taylor series of the exponential in Eq. (
7.3.26
) up to the second order in
\alpha_{0}
and sum up in the two polarizations explicitly:

u_{em}\simeq\sum_{n}\int\frac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{dt}{t}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-t\left(k^{2}+n^{2}\pi^{2}/L^{2}\right)}\left[2+t^{2}\alpha_{0}^{2}\left(k^{2}+\frac{n^{2}\pi^{2}}{L^{2}}\right)\right]\frac{1}{\Gamma(-\frac{1}{2})}.

(7.3.27)

As it is usually done in calculations with integrals, as in Eq. (
7.3.27
), we carry out the Gaussian integration over k, adopt the Euler representation of the Gamma function, carry out the sum over
n
via the definition of the Riemann zeta function
[
113
]
, and we finally obtain

u_{em}\simeq u_{0}(L,D)+u_{a}(L,D,\alpha_{0}),

(7.3.28)

where

\displaystyle u_{0}(L,D)=-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{D/2}}\left(\frac{\pi}{L}\right)^{D+1}\Gamma\left(-\frac{D+1}{2}\right)\zeta(-D-1),

(7.3.29a)

\displaystyle u_{a}(L,D,\alpha_{0})=-\frac{\alpha^{2}_{0}}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{D/2}}\left(\frac{\pi}{L}\right)^{D-1}\left[\Gamma\left(\frac{3-D}{2}\right)\zeta(-D-1)+\frac{D}{2}\,\Gamma\left(\frac{1-D}{2}\right)\zeta(-D+1)\right].

(7.3.29b)

The first term
u_{0}
is the familiar result of the Casimir energy, while
u_{a}
is the second-order contribution. For
D=2
we obtain the Casimir force per unit area

\displaystyle f_{0}(L)=-\frac{\pi^{2}}{240}\frac{1}{L^{4}}\,\,\,,\qquad f_{a}(L,\alpha_{0})=-\frac{7\alpha^{2}_{0}}{320}\frac{1}{L^{2}}\,\,\,.

(7.3.30a)

The second-order correction, going as
\sim\frac{1}{L^{2}}
, is similar to what was obtained in Ref.
[
169
]
, although our result differs by a multiplicative factor of
0.63
.

There are some relevant points about the possibilities of physical application of these results:

•

The approximation
\dot{\Theta}\sim\alpha_{0}
can be used to approximate a coherently oscillating axion field for very small times
t\ll\omega_{a}^{-1}
, meaning that the spectral theory is a good approximation for frequencies
\omega\gg\omega_{a}
. This means that it can be adopted when the frequency contributing more significantly to the Casimir force between the plates, which is roughly
\sim L^{-1}
, is much larger than the axion frequency, so
{L}\ll\omega_{a}^{-1}
. We have nowadays measurements with percent-level accuracy of the Casimir pressure between parallel plates in the range
d\sim 160
-750 nm
[
99
,
100
]
. This means it is a good approximation for the dark matter QCD axion and ALPS with mass
\lesssim 2\,\rm{eV}
.

•

In order the axion correction to be comparable with the usual Casimir pressure in our current Universe with
\Theta_{0}\sim 10^{-19}

[
238
]
, it needs to be of the order of
L=L_{a}\sim(\Theta_{0}\,\omega_{a})^{-1}\sim 10^{14}\,m\left(\frac{10^{-2}$\text{\,}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}$}{m_{a}}\right)
and this is not viable with possible experiments and the former mass range.

•

It can be of interest for topological insulators, since
b_{0}\sim 10^{-1}\,$\text{\,}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}$
, corresponding to
\alpha_{0}\sim 10^{-1}\,$\text{\,}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}$
with now
L_{a}\sim$2\text{\,}\,\mu\mathrm{m}$
, as a percent-level correction.
It can be of further theoretical interest for applications to vacuum fluctuations in the Early Universe, where we can expect values
\alpha_{0}\sim g_{\gamma}m_{a}\sim 10^{-2}\,m_{a}
.

The toy model
7.4.1
, which we will develop in the following, is very good for catching the fundamental aspects of the optical properties of the real axion domain walls, since the only ways they could differ are in the expected "smoother" interfaces for the real domain wall. Such effects involve considering the possibility of further multiple reflections and transmissions in the limited region near to interfaces, which are subleading if the reflection coefficients depend on
g_{a\gamma\gamma}
and then the optical depth is small.

7.3.2
High frequency approximation for the Green’s function

Before working out the case of an oscillating axion field, we can get some quantitative understanding in the following way. We can substitute
\Theta(t)=\Theta_{0}\sin{(\omega_a t)}
in Eq. (
7.2.2
), and calculate the Fourier Transform

(-\omega^{2}+|\vec{k}|^{2})\tilde{G}_{ij}(\omega,\vec{k})-i\epsilon_{lik}k_{l}\frac{\Theta_{0}\omega_{a}}{2}\left[\tilde{G}_{ij}(\omega+\omega_{a},\vec{k})+\tilde{G}_{ij}(\omega-\omega_{a},\vec{k})\right]=\delta_{ij},

(7.3.31)

where we have used the modulation property of the Fourier Transform
[
131
,
241
]
.
The high-frequency approximation consists of assuming
\omega\gg\omega_{a}
.
In this way, up to the first order in
\omega_{a}
, the Green’s function satisfies the following equation,

(-\omega^{2}+|\vec{k}|^{2})\tilde{G}_{ij}(\omega,\vec{k})-i\epsilon_{lik}\,k_{l}\,\Theta_{0}\,\omega_{a}\tilde{G}_{kj}(\omega,\vec{k})=\delta_{ij}.

(7.3.32)

It has the same form as the previous equation for (
7.3.2
) and has accordingly the same solutions, with just the substitution
\dot{\Theta}\rightarrow\Theta_{0}\omega_{a}
.
The interpretation of Eq. (
7.3.12
) terms is still valid in this case.

7.3.3
Case with an oscillating axion field: Production of real photons

In order to treat this case properly we need to work out Eq. (
7.3.1
) in more detail.

We assume that the electromagnetic wave vector is directed along a fixed direction, without losing generality. Actually, the axion field is approximated to be dependent on time but spatially homogeneous, so it is isotropic.

We perform a Fourier Transform in space domain and put for simplicity
\vec{k}=|\vec{k}|\hat{e}_{z}
, since we are in the isotropic vacuum, so we can rotate our
z
axis in the direction of
\vec{k}
.

We expand the formalism up to second order in
\Theta_{0}
and consider
G_{xy}
and
G_{yy}
, whose equations are coupled only between themselves.
To order zero, we have

\begin{split}G_{yy}(\vec{k},t,t^{\prime})&=\theta_{H}(t-t^{\prime})\;\times\frac{1}{|\vec{k}|}\,\sin\left[|\vec{k}|(t-t^{\prime})\right],\\
G_{xy}(\vec{k},t,t^{\prime})&=0,\end{split}

(7.3.33)

according to the null initial conditions we adopt, where the function
\theta_{H}(t-t^{\prime})
is the usual Heaviside function.

We can solve the resulting D’Alembert equation and specify the time dependence of the axion field in order to obtain as in Ref.
[
123
]
the following perturbative expansion in Fourier space

\tilde{G}_{jk}(\vec{k},\omega)\sim\tilde{G}^{(0)}_{jk}(\vec{k},\omega)+\tilde{G}^{(1)}_{jk}(\vec{k},\omega)+\tilde{G}^{(2)}_{jk}(\vec{k},\omega),

(7.3.34)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{G}^{(0)}_{jk}(\vec{k},\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\omega^{2}-k^{2})},

(7.3.35a)

\displaystyle\tilde{G}^{(1)}_{jk}(\vec{k},\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{(\omega^{2}-k^{2})^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{(\omega-\omega_{a})^{2}-|\vec{k}|^{2}}+\frac{1}{(\omega+\omega_{a})^{2}-|\vec{k}|^{2}}\right]\epsilon_{jkl}\beta_{l},

(7.3.35b)

\displaystyle\tilde{G}^{(2)}_{jk}(\vec{k},\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta^{2}}{(\omega^{2}-k^{2})}\left[\frac{1}{(\omega-\omega_{a})^{2}-|\vec{k}|^{2}}+\frac{1}{(\omega+\omega_{a})^{2}-|\vec{k}|^{2}}\right]\left[\delta_{jk}+\frac{\epsilon_{jkl}\beta_{l}}{\omega^{2}-k^{2}}\right]+

(7.3.35c)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\omega^{2}-k^{2})}\left[\frac{1}{(\omega-\omega_{a})^{2}-|\vec{k}|^{2}}+\frac{1}{(\omega+\omega_{a})^{2}-|\vec{k}|^{2}}\right]^{2}\Theta_{0}^{2}\omega_{a}^{2}k_{j}k_{k}.

(7.3.35d)

It is worth mentioning that the results for axion oscillating background are compatible with models of axion echo, treated recently in Ref.
[
20
]
.

Figure 7.3:
Plot of the function
f(\omega,\Theta_{0},\omega_{a})
in Eq. (
7.3.37
) with
\Theta_{0}=1
for simplicity of visualization.
For
\omega>\omega_{a}
we have the expected behaviour from Eq. (
7.3.12
), whose infrared cut-off is the peak at
\omega=\omega_{a}
, associated with the limiting case of the decay of one axion to only one detectable photon. Furthermore, we have a peak at
\omega=\frac{\omega_{a}}{2}
, a result compatible with the axion echo phenomenon
[
20
]
, where the physical process behind is the decay of an axion of energy
\omega_{a}
to two photons with energy
\frac{\omega_{a}}{2}
.

It is also possible in this case to evaluate the second-order contribution to energy density:

\rho^{(2)}(\omega)\;d\omega=d\omega\;\omega^{2}\left[\frac{1}{2}f(\omega,\Theta_{0},\omega_{a})+\frac{1}{e^{\omega/T}-1}f(\omega,\Theta_{0},\omega_{a})\right],

(7.3.36)

where

f(\omega,\Theta_{0},\omega_{a})=\frac{\Theta_{0}^{2}\omega_{a}^{2}\omega(8\omega^{4}+\omega_{a}^{4})}{2(\omega+\omega_{a})|\omega-\omega_{a}|(\omega_{a}^{2}-4\omega^{2})^{2}}.

(7.3.37)

A numerical plot of the function
f
is displayed in Figure
7.3
. From this plot, we observe how the validity of an expression of Casimir force per unit area between two conducting plates analogous to the case of a time-increasing axion field is true for
L^{-1}\gg\omega_{a}
, as mentioned before. For a bigger
L
, one appreciates the deviations from that case and considers that the behaviour of the modification to energy density going as
1/\omega
needs to be corrected with a
1/|\omega-\omega_{a}|
factor and has a lower cut-off at
\omega=\omega_{a}
. Furthermore, one needs to take care of the additional contribution of "axion echo"
[
20
]
at
\omega=\frac{\omega_{a}}{2}
.

This last contribution has a straightforward physical interpretation: it is associated with the production of virtual photons with frequency
\omega=\frac{\omega_{a}}{2}
from the decay of an axion, while the peak at
\omega=\omega_{a}
is associated with a decay of one axion to only one detectable photon.

7.3.4
A possible way to boost axionic Casimir effect in a current Universe experimental set-up

In this subsection, we consider a dielectric system containing two interfaces separating media of refractive indices
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
in a uniform magnetic field
B_{e}
. We assume that the media are elastic, so that medium 2 can be "turned back" and glued to the left side of medium 1. This is a ring-formed system that we have treated in Ref.
[
63
]
and extended in Ref.
[
123
]
.

Figure 7.4:
Geometry and notation of the closed string of interest. Figure from Refs.
[
63
,
123
]
.

Figure
7.4
shows the configuration, and here we give the most fundamental results.

We take
\sigma
to be the length coordinate along the string, such that the two junctions are at
\sigma=0
and
\sigma=L_{1}
, where
L_{1}
is the length of region I. The total length of the string is
L=L_{1}+L_{2}
, where
L_{2}
is the length of region II, and the junctions
\sigma=0
and
\sigma=L
are coincident. The string lies in the
xy
plane, and a strong uniform magnetic field
{\bf B}_{e}
is applied in the
z
direction.

We evaluate the stationary oscillations of the electromagnetic oscillations in the string. If
E_{I}(\sigma,t)
and
E_{II}(\sigma,t)
are the electric fields in the two regions, we have, using a complex representation,

\displaystyle E_{I}(\sigma,t)=\xi_{I}e^{in_{1}\omega\sigma-i\omega t}+\eta_{I}e^{-in_{1}\omega\sigma-i\omega t},

(7.3.38a)

\displaystyle E_{II}(\sigma,t)=\xi_{II}e^{in_{2}\omega(\sigma-L_{1})-i\omega t}+\eta_{II}e^{-in_{2}\omega(\sigma-L_{1})-i\omega t},

(7.3.38b)

where
\xi_{I},\eta_{I},\xi_{II},\eta_{II}
are constants. Analogously, we obtain the magnetic fields.
One could treat such a system by considering a generic
\omega
, however, as we discussed in Ref.
[
63
]
, the axion-generated oscillations would be suppressed by a factor
\delta(\omega-\omega_{a})
, so the only relevant frequency is
\omega_{a}
for our purposes.

By imposing the appropriate boundary conditions at the junctions, we can get analytical expressions for particular cases of interest, namely when
n_{2}
becomes large in comparison to
n_{1}
so that the ratio
x=\frac{n_{1}}{n_{2}}\rightarrow 0
and when the length
L_{I}\rightarrow 0
, corresponding to a point defect sitting on an otherwise uniform string. As
\delta_{1}\rightarrow 0
in this case, we see that

\xi_{I}=\frac{E_{0}}{2\varepsilon_{1}},\quad x\rightarrow 0,L_{I}\rightarrow 0,

(7.3.39)

which is a real quantity.
We also have for this last case:

\displaystyle\eta_{I}=\xi_{I},

(7.3.40)

\displaystyle\eta_{II}=\xi_{II}=0.

(7.3.41)

We have then an electromagnetic energy density
u_{1def}
inside the point defect:

u_{1def}=\frac{E_{0}^{2}}{4\epsilon_{1}}.

(7.3.42)

There are interesting similarities and differences with the system of a single dielectric plate treated in Ref.
[
193
]
.
Indeed, our system is similar to it, but when the two outward dielectric media are closed in order to form a ring.

We have suggested in Ref.
[
123
]
the possibility that, adopting the same idea of Ref.
[
193
]
with a ring of multiple dielectrics, we can boost the stationary oscillations and adopt it for experimental purposes.

7.4
Optical properties of domain walls

The purpose of this subsection is to study the propagation of an electromagnetic wave in the presence of a toy model for the axion domain wall. The toy wall is taken to be a planar sheet in which the axion field increases linearly with the longitudinal coordinate
z
between
z=0
and
z=L
and it is constant and uniform outside. We assume a static wall, so
a=a(z)
with

\Theta(z)=\begin{cases}0\;\;\;\textit{if}\;\;\;z<0,\\
\frac{\Theta_{0}}{L}z\;\;\;\textit{if}\;\;\;0<z<L,\\
\Theta_{0}\;\;\;\textit{if}\;\;\;z>L.\end{cases}

(7.4.1)

The model was treated earlier in Ref.
[
67
]
, and we extended it to use as a toy model for "localised" axion configurations, in particular the axion domain walls in Section (
5.4.3
).

Figure 7.5:
Explicit graph of the static axion domain wall configuration
\Theta(z)=g_{a\gamma\gamma}a_{\rm phys}(z)
where
a_{\rm phys}(z)=\gamma_{0}(z)+\alpha_{0}(z)
takes care of respectively pion and
\eta
contribution. It is reported in the solid blue line, and evaluated as in Ref.
[
152
]
. We also reported the corresponding values for
\gamma_{0}(z)
and
\alpha_{0}(z)
.

We will now investigate the effects of the axion on the electromagnetic wave in the system.
As shown in Ref.
[
152
]
the ’effective field’ in Axion-Modified Maxwell equations for a QCD axion domain wall gets two contributions: one
\alpha_{0}(z)
from the phase of Peccei-Quinn field, i.e. the axion field and
\gamma_{0}(z)
from the neutral pion field, since the pion has an interaction term to electromagnetic field analogous to Eq. (
6.1.1
).
The two contributions are related because of the equilibrium condition for a static wall:

\tan{\gamma_0}=-\xi\tan{\alpha_0},

(7.4.2)

where
\xi=\frac{m_{u}-m_{d}}{m_{u}+m_{d}}
, where
m_{u}
is the mass of the up quark and
m_{d}
is the mass of down quark.
In our numerical evaluation here we used the value
\xi=0.3
as in the original paper and we evaluated
\alpha_{0}(z)
by solving numerically the equation:

\frac{1}{m_{a}^{2}}\frac{d^{2}\alpha_{0}}{dz^{2}}=\frac{\sin{\alpha_0}\cos{\alpha_0}}{\sqrt{\cos^{2}{\alpha_{0}}+\xi^{2}\sin^{2}{\alpha_{0}}}},

(7.4.3)

We plot one of the possible solutions of Eq. (
7.4.3
) in Figure
7.5
.

7.4.1
Exact calculations for toy model

As above, we consider an axion configuration that is dependent on
z
only. At first, we assume
\beta(z)=\partial_{z}\Theta(z)
to be an arbitrary function of
z
.
The incident electromagnetic wave is propagating along the
z
-axis and is transversely polarized (the same configuration was assumed in Ref.
[
152
]
).
This configuration is useful because a domain wall is invariant under Lorentz boosts parallel to the wall surface, so it is always possible to find a reference frame where the wave is incident normally, as also noticed in the same
[
152
]
.

This simplifies the calculations to the first order in the 4-potential because the effective axion charge density is zero.
We then consider the following equation:

\Box\vec{A}=-\nabla\Theta\wedge\frac{\partial\vec{A}}{\partial t},

(7.4.4)

and search for an exact solution.
We calculate the Fourier transform of this equation in time
t
and in the
x
and
y
coordinates, and get the following expressions for the FT components of the vector potential:

\begin{split}(-\partial_{z}^{2}-\kappa^{2})\mathcal{A}_{x}&=i\omega\beta(z)\mathcal{A}_{y},\\
(-\partial_{z}^{2}-\kappa^{2})\mathcal{A}_{y}&=-i\omega\beta(z)\mathcal{A}_{x},\\
(-\partial_{z}^{2}-\kappa^{2})\mathcal{A}_{z}&=0.\end{split}

(7.4.5)

When
k_{x}=k_{y}=0
, we have
\kappa=\omega
.
Equations (
7.4.5
) imply that the gauge fields defined as
\mathcal{A}_{\pm}=\mathcal{A}_{x}\pm i\mathcal{A}_{y}
satisfy:

\begin{split}(-\partial_{z}^{2}-\kappa^{2})\mathcal{A}_{\pm}&=\pm\omega\beta(z)\mathcal{A}_{\pm}.\end{split}

(7.4.6)

From now on, we will assume to deal with the toy model configuration (
7.4.1
).
We notice that the axion configuration (
7.4.1
) does not have well-defined values of the z derivative at
z=0,L
since the derivative has a jump in these points. However, this is not problematic for treating Eq. (
7.4.4
) since vector potential and its first spatial derivative are continuous.

Indeed, in the following calculations, we basically solve Eq. (
7.4.4
) separately for the regions
z<0
,
z>L
and
0<z<L
and then impose continuity of vector potential and its first z-derivative.
This last procedure would also be good in the case of an exact domain wall, as an approximation.

We consider a plane wave propagating along z.
Then, the equations (
7.4.6
) can be interpreted as follows.

The gauge fields
\mathcal{A}_{\pm}
correspond to left/right circular polarizations, then this means that the phase velocities are different for a left circular polarized wave and a right one, and the optical angle rotates inside the slab.

We now evaluate the reflected component of an incident orthogonal wave. The continuity conditions at the walls are that the
A_{\pm}
fields, as well as their
z
derivatives, are continuous. Thus, in the leftmost region,

\mathcal{A}_{\pm}(z)_{\text{left}}=A_{\pm}e^{-i\kappa z}+B_{\pm}e^{i\kappa z},

(7.4.7)

where the
B
term is the reflected wave. The wave transmitted outside the slab is

\mathcal{A}_{\pm}(z)_{\text{right}}=C_{\pm}e^{-i\kappa z}.

(7.4.8)

A_{\pm}(z)_{\text{in}}=A^{\prime}_{\pm}e^{-i\alpha_{\pm}z}+B^{\prime}_{\pm}e^{i\alpha_{\pm}z}.

(7.4.9)

where
A^{\prime}_{\pm}
is the wave transmitted inside the wall.
To evaluate the reflection coefficient (defined as
R_{\pm}=\frac{B_{\pm}}{A_{\pm}})
and the internal transmission coefficient, defined as
T_{\pm}=\frac{A^{\prime}_{\pm}}{A_{\pm}}
, we need to impose the continuity conditions on the surfaces
z=0
and
z=L
:

\begin{cases}A+B=A^{\prime}+B^{\prime}&\text{Continuity of $\mathcal{A}_{\pm}$ at z=0},\\
\omega A-\omega B=\alpha A^{\prime}-\alpha B^{\prime}&\text{Continuity of first derivatives of $\mathcal{A}_{\pm}$ at z=0},\\
e^{-i\alpha L}A^{\prime}+e^{i\alpha L}B^{\prime}=Ce^{-i\omega L}&\text{Continuity of $\mathcal{A}_{\pm}$ at z=L},\\
-i\alpha e^{-i\alpha L}A^{\prime}+i\alpha e^{i\alpha L}B^{\prime}=-i\omega Ce^{-i\omega L}&\text{Continuity of first derivatives of $\mathcal{A}_{\pm}$ at z=L}.\end{cases}

(7.4.10)

From it, we get

\displaystyle T_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\frac{2e^{2i\alpha_{\pm}L}\omega(\alpha_{\pm}+\omega)}{(-1+e^{2i\alpha_{\pm}L})\alpha^{2}+(-1+e^{2i\alpha L})\omega^{2}+2(1+e^{2i\alpha_{\pm}L})\alpha_{\pm}\omega},

(7.4.11a)

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=-\frac{(-1+e^{2i\alpha_{\pm}L})(\alpha^{2}_{\pm}-\omega^{2})}{(-1+e^{2i\alpha L})\alpha^{2}_{\pm}+(-1+e^{2i\alpha_{\pm}L})\omega^{2}+2(1+e^{2i\alpha L})\alpha_{\pm}\omega}.

(7.4.11b)

Some fundamental properties of the reflection coefficient are shown in Figs. (
7.6
) and (
7.7
). Modules and phases are given, where we have taken the unit frequency to be
1/L
and
\Theta_{0}=10^{-3}
for simplicity.

We notice that there is the most reflection, for both polarizations, when
\omega=0
and
\omega=\frac{\Theta_{0}}{L}
, but there is still reflection at higher frequencies at intervals of
\omega=\frac{\Theta_{0}}{L}
.

Figure 7.6:
Plot of reflection coefficient modulus for the two circular polarizations.

Figure 7.7:
Plot of the reflection coefficient phase for the two circular polarizations.

In the following, we will adopt approximative expressions for the reflection coefficients
R_{\pm}(\kappa)
.
For simplicity we define the useful quantity
m_{L}=\frac{\Theta_{0}}{L}
.

The reflection coefficients can be approximated to be
R^{2}_{+}(k_{z})\sim\zeta m_{L}\,\delta(k_{z})
and
R^{2}_{-}(k_{z})\sim\zeta\,m_{L}\,\left[\delta(k_{z}+m_{L})+\delta(k_{z}-m_{L})\right]
where
\zeta
is a numerical factor of order of unity to fit better the behaviour of the reflection coefficients.
This can be justified by noticing that, although the exact behaviours of the square modula of
R_{\pm}(k_{z})
are complicated, it is clear that a crucial frequency for our system is the same
m_{L}
, and by the graphs in Figures (
7.6
) and (
7.7
) and a qualitative description of them, they can be approximated to be
R^{2}_{+}(k_{z})\sim\frac{\zeta}{\pi}\Theta_{0}^{2}m_{L}^{2}\frac{\sin^{2}{(k_{z}/m_{L})}}{k^{2}_{z}}
and
R^{2}_{-}(k_{z})\sim\frac{\zeta}{\pi}\Theta_{0}^{2}\frac{\sin^{2}{(k_{z}/m_{L}-1)}}{(k_{z}/m_{L}-1)^{2}}
.
Then one exploits the distributional relation:

\frac{\sin^{2}{(\epsilon t)}}{t^{2}}\sim\pi\,\epsilon\,\delta(t)\qquad\text{for}\qquad\epsilon\rightarrow 0,

(7.4.12)

and we can get the former expressions posing
\epsilon=(m_{L})^{-1}
.

The adoption of the Dirac delta limit is only for simplicity and takes care of the main contribution coming from the main peak of Figure (
7.6
), however, an exact calculation would correspond to taking care of the secondary peaks of the graphs in Figure (
7.6
) along with their detailed structure, from which we adopt the numerical factor
\zeta
.

7.4.2
Dispersion relation

In the previous section we obtained the following dispersion relation for the toy model (
7.4.1
):

\omega^{2}-k_{z}^{2}=\mp\omega\beta(z),

(7.4.13)

implying

\omega_{\pm}=\sqrt{\left(k_{z}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\beta^{2}(z)\right)}\pm\frac{1}{2}\beta(z).

(7.4.14)

This is the same dispersion relation as obtained in Ref.
[
67
]
, which is not the general dispersion relation.
This can be understood from our discussion in the previous section, since it applies to just one linear polarization in the reference frame where the wave vector lies along the z direction.

We obtain the general form by applying an inverse Lorentz boost in x and y directions and obtain the following form, similar to the one in Ref.
[
128
]
:

\omega=\sqrt{k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}+\left(\sqrt{\left(k_{z}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\beta^{2}_{z}\right)}\pm\frac{1}{2}\beta_{z}\right)^{2}}.

(7.4.15)

Having in mind this full expression, we can discuss it physically. It tells us that we have two different dispersion relations between
+
and
-
polarization and this lead to a rotation of the optical angle.

7.4.3
Optical activity of axion medium and practical application

It is worth mentioning that the mode splittings in the dispersion relations obtained in Subsections
7.3.1
and
7.4.2
are analogous to those in the Faraday effect, where the polarization rotation is proportional to the longitudinal strong magnetic field
[
220
,
185
]
.
Moreover, there is also a strong connection with the Casimir polarization rotation observed in a chiral medium when a strong transverse magnetic field is present

[
162
,
154
]
.

This effect has been treated in much detail for Axion Electrodynamics with only an axion field that holds constant spatial gradients and time derivative, in Refs.
[
238
]
and
[
77
]
. It is indeed easy to notice how our theory with a fixed axion background is equivalent to a modified Electrodynamics with an additional Chern-Simons term:

\mathcal{L}_{a}=-\frac{1}{2}p_{\alpha}A_{\beta}\tilde{F}^{\alpha\beta},

(7.4.16)

if
p_{\alpha}=g_{a\gamma\gamma}\partial_{\alpha}a(x)
, regardless if axion derivatives are constant and uniform or not.

The theory of this modified electrodynamics when
p_{\alpha}
are constants is very well treated there, where they find that the Axion Electrodynamics medium is characterized by having an optically active vacuum with constant rotation of the optical angle

\frac{d\Phi}{dt}=\frac{1}{2}g_{a\gamma\gamma}\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\varepsilon}}\left(\dot{a}+\frac{\omega}{k^{2}}\vec{k}\cdot\nabla a\right).

(7.4.17)

This comes from the dispersion relation:

\omega_{\pm}=\frac{|\vec{k}|}{\sqrt{\varepsilon\mu}}\pm\frac{1}{2}g_{a\gamma\gamma}\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\varepsilon}}\left(\dot{a}+\frac{\omega}{|\vec{k}|^{2}}\vec{k}\cdot\nabla a\right)+\mathcal{O}(g_{a\gamma\gamma}^{2}),

(7.4.18)

which is compatible with our results.

We have used this idea in Ref.
[
64
]
for the following system of interest for topological materials.

7.4.4
Application to topological material

We consider a usual system with planar symmetry along a z-axis.
We consider the regions
z<L
and
z>0
to be perfectly conducting, and the intermediate region
0<z<L
to be filled with a uniform dielectric with material constants
\varepsilon
and
\mu
